|\langle\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\xi,\cdot),\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle|\lesssim\|\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\xi,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\|\frac{1}{|\xi|\langle r\rangle\langle r\xi\rangle^{1/2}}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\frac{1}{|\xi|^{3/2}}

Using that
\xi\mapsto 1-\chi(\xi)
is supported in
\{|\xi|\geq 1\}
, we get that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}^{R}(\phi)(\xi)=(1-\chi(\xi))\langle\phi\,,\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{b}(\xi,r)\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
satisfies

(5.9)

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}^{R}(\phi)\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}^{-1R}
from
L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, (
1.11
) and (
1.13
),

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\sharp}\zeta(r)|

\displaystyle=|\langle\zeta,(1-\chi(\xi))\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\xi,r)\lambda^{\prime}\operatorname{sign}\xi\rangle_{L^{2}(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi}{\pi})}|\lesssim\|\zeta\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\|(1-\chi(\xi))\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\xi,r)\lambda^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi}{|\xi|})}

(5.10)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|(1-\chi(\xi))\frac{1}{\langle r\rangle\langle\xi r\rangle^{1/2}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi}{|\xi|})}\lesssim\mathbb{1}(r\leq 1)\langle\ln r\rangle+\mathbbm{1}(r>1)r^{-\frac{3}{2}}.

Thus,

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}^{-1R}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1.

Step 3: Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{S}_{\flat}
from
L^{2}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}L^{2}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
and from
L^{2}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
L^{2}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
,

and of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1S}_{\flat}
from
L^{2}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
and from
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}L^{2}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}
from
L^{2}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}L^{2}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
. Pick
\phi=(\varphi,-\varphi)^{\top}
with
\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=1
. We have by (
1.4
) that for
\xi>0

(5.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}\phi(\xi)=\xi\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}m^{S}_{\flat}(\xi,r)\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

where, introducing
\widetilde{b}_{\flat}(\xi)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}b_{\flat}(\xi)
,

(5.12)

\displaystyle m_{\flat}^{S}(\xi,r)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{b}_{\flat}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)J_{0}(\xi r)+c_{\flat}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)\frac{\sin(\xi r-\frac{\pi}{4})}{\sqrt{\xi r}}(1-\chi(r))

\displaystyle\qquad+\widetilde{b}_{\flat}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)(\rho(r)-1)\chi(\xi r)

\displaystyle\qquad+c_{\flat}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)\sin\left(\xi r-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi r}}\left(\chi(r)-\chi(\xi r)\right)\

\displaystyle=I+II+III+IV

For the first and second terms, we have that the functions
\xi\mapsto\widetilde{b}_{\flat}(\xi)\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)
and
\xi\mapsto c_{\flat}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)
are continuous at the origin. Notice that for a radial function
v
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then
\xi\mapsto\langle J_{0}(\xi\cdot),v\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
is the standard two-dimensional Fourier transform of
v
. Notice also that
\xi\mapsto\langle\frac{\cos(\xi\cdot)+i\sin(\xi\cdot)}{\sqrt{\cdot}},v\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
is the standard one-dimensional Fourier transform of the function
x\mapsto\mathbbm{1}(x>0)\sqrt{|x|}v(|x|)
. Therefore, the Plancherel theorem in one and two dimensions imply that

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}(I(\xi,r)+II(\xi,r))\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1.

Then, using that
|\rho(r)-1|\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-2}
, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}III(\xi,r)\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right|\lesssim\|\langle r\rangle^{-2}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1,

from which there follows that
\|\int_{0}^{\infty}III(\xi,r)\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
.

Using
|c_{\flat}(\xi)|\lesssim|\xi|
we have
|IV|\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\xi}}{\sqrt{r}}(\chi(\xi r)-\chi(r))
hence

\left\|IV\right\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\left[\int\frac{\xi}{r}|\chi(\xi r)|^{2}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right]^{1/2}\lesssim 1

so that, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get
\|\int_{0}^{\infty}IV(\xi,r)\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
. Combining all these estimates, we find that

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}\phi_{o}\|_{\xi\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}=\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}m^{S}_{\flat}(\xi,r)\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}
from
L^{2}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
L^{2}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
. Pick
\phi\in L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=1
(which for our purpose does actually not need to be even). We have that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}\phi(\xi)=\chi(\xi)\langle\psi^{S}_{\flat}(\xi,\cdot),\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
where by (
1.3
) that for
\xi>0
, we decompose

(5.13)

\displaystyle\chi(\xi)\psi_{\flat}^{S}(\xi,r)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{b}_{\flat}\chi(\xi)J_{0}(\xi r)e(\xi)+c_{\flat}\chi(\xi)\frac{\sin(\xi r-\frac{\pi}{4})}{\sqrt{\xi r}}(1-\chi(r))e(\xi)

\displaystyle\qquad+\widetilde{b}_{\flat}\chi(\xi)(\rho(r)-1)\chi(\xi r)e(\xi)+c_{\flat}\chi(\xi)\sin\left(\xi r-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi r}}(\chi-\chi_{\xi^{-1}})e(\xi)\

\displaystyle=I+II+III+IV.

The contributions of
I
,
II
,
III
and
IV
can be treated exactly as the contributions of the corresponding terms in (
5.12
), and we obtain
\left\|\langle I+II+III+IV,\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\right\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
, implying
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}\phi\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
as desired.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}
from
L^{2}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. Pick an even function of the form
\zeta(\xi)=\varphi(|\xi|)
with
\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}((0,\infty),|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}=1
. We have by (
1.3
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}\zeta(r)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\zeta(\xi)\psi_{\flat,e}^{S}(\xi,r)\chi(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}(\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi=\int_{0}^{\infty}m^{-1S}_{\flat}(\xi,r)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

where
\psi_{\flat,e}^{S}=\frac{1}{2}(\psi_{\flat}^{S}(\xi,r)+\sigma_{1}\psi_{\flat}^{S}(\xi,r))
and where, using
\psi_{\flat}(-\xi)=-\sigma_{1}\psi_{\flat}(-\xi)
and
\sigma_{1}e(\xi)=-e(-\xi)
,

(5.14)

\displaystyle m_{\flat}^{-1S}(\xi,r)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{b}_{\flat}\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\frac{e(\xi)-e(-\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)J_{0}(\xi r)+c_{\flat}\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\frac{e(\xi)-e(-\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)\frac{\sin(\xi r-\frac{\pi}{4})}{\sqrt{\xi r}}(1-\chi(r))

\displaystyle\qquad+\widetilde{b}_{\flat}\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\frac{e(\xi)-e(-\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)(\rho(r)-1)\chi(\xi r)

\displaystyle\qquad+c_{\flat}\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\frac{e(\xi)-e(-\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)\sin(\xi r-\frac{\pi}{4})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi r}}\left(\chi(r)-\chi(\xi r)\right)\

\displaystyle=I+II+III+IV

The first and second terms can be dealt with as for the first and second terms in (
5.12
), using that
\xi\mapsto\frac{e(\xi)-e(-\xi)}{\xi}
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are smooth at the origin, appealing to the
L^{2}
continuity of the inverse Fourier transform in two and one dimensions, and we obtain
\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}(I(\xi,r)+II(\xi,r))\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
. Then, using
|\rho(r)-1|\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-2}
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}III(\xi,r)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right|\lesssim\|\chi(\xi)(\rho-1)\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle r\rangle^{2}}

from which there follows that
\|\int_{0}^{\infty}III(\xi,r)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
. For
r>1
, using
|IV|\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{\xi}}{\sqrt{r}}\chi(\xi r)
we have

\left\|IV\right\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\left[\int_{0}^{2}\frac{\xi}{r}\chi_{\xi^{-1}}^{2}(r)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right]^{1/2}\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{2}}

so that
\|\int_{0}^{\infty}IV(\xi,r)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
by Cauchy-Schwarz. Combining these estimates, we find that
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}\zeta_{o}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1S}_{\flat}
from
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}L^{2}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. Pick a function
\zeta(\xi)=|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\varphi(\xi)
(which for our purpose actually does not need to be odd) with
\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}=1
. We have by (
1.3
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}\zeta(r)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi_{\flat}^{S}(\xi,r)\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\chi(\xi)\varphi(\xi)|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

We decompose
\chi(\xi)\psi_{\flat}^{S}
for
\xi>0
according to (
5.13
), and for
\xi<0
we also use the same decomposition since
\psi^{S}_{\flat}(-\xi)=-\sigma_{1}\psi^{S}_{\flat}(\xi)
. The terms
I
,
II
,
III
and
IV
can be treated exactly as the terms
I
,
II
,
III
and
IV
in (
5.14
) were treated above, so that
\|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(I+II+III+IV)(\xi,r)\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
. We have showed
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
.

Step 4: Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{R}_{\flat}
from
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
. Recall that

\frac{1}{\xi}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{R}(\phi)(\xi)=\chi(\xi)\langle\frac{\psi_{\flat}^{R}(\xi,\cdot)}{\xi},\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}.

Pick
\phi\in L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
. We have by (
1.6
)

(5.15)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi}\langle\psi_{\flat}^{R},\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=\left\langle\frac{m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}}{\xi}\chi(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}+\left\langle\frac{m_{\flat,1}^{R}}{\xi}\cos(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}+\{\mbox{similar}\}.

Let
|\xi|\leq 2
. For the first term, we have
\||\xi|^{-1}m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
by (
1.7
), and for the second we have
\||\xi|^{-1}m_{\flat,1}^{R}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1+\ln^{2}|\xi|
by (
1.8
). By Cauchy-Schwarz this implies
||\xi|^{-1}\langle\psi_{\flat}^{R},\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}|\lesssim 1+\ln^{2}|\xi|
so we deduce that
\||\xi|^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{R}(\phi)(\xi)\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
as desired.

Step 5: Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\flat}
from
L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. Recall that

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\flat}(\zeta)(r)=\langle\zeta,\chi(\xi)\psi_{\flat}^{R}(\xi,r)\lambda^{\prime}\operatorname{sign}\xi\rangle_{L^{2}(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi}{\pi})}.

Pick
\zeta\in L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
with
\|\zeta\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
. We have by (
1.6
)

(5.16)

\displaystyle\pi\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\flat}(\zeta)(r)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\zeta,\chi(\xi)m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}\chi(\xi r)\lambda^{\prime}\operatorname{sign}\xi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\langle\zeta,\chi(\xi)m_{\flat,1}^{R}\cos(\xi r)\lambda^{\prime}\operatorname{sign}\xi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}+\mbox{similar}.

For the first term, we have, using (
1.7
) and
|\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)|\lesssim 1
for
|\xi|\leq 2
, that
\||\xi|^{-1/2}\chi(\xi)m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}\chi(\xi r)\lambda^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-3}
, so that we have using
\||\xi|^{1/2}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
and Cauchy-Schwarz
|\langle\zeta,\chi(\xi)m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}\chi(\xi r)\lambda^{\prime}\operatorname{sign}\xi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}|\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-3}
. Hence

(5.17)

\left\|\left\langle\zeta,\chi(\xi)m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}\chi(\xi r)\lambda^{\prime}\operatorname{sign}\xi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\right\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\|\langle r\rangle^{-3}\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1.

For the second term, we have, using (
1.8
) that
\||\xi|^{-1/2}\chi(\xi)m_{\flat,1}^{R}\cos(\xi r)\lambda^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-3/2}\langle\ln\langle r\rangle\rangle^{2}
, so that we have by Cauchy-Schwarz

\left\|\langle\zeta,\chi(\xi)m_{\flat,1}^{R}\cos(\xi r)\lambda^{\prime}\operatorname{sign}\xi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\right\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\||\xi|^{1/2}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\|\langle r\rangle^{-3/2}\langle\ln\langle r\rangle\rangle^{2}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1.

Combining the above bounds, we find the desired estimate
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\flat}(\zeta)\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
.
∎

5.5.
Weighted
L^{2}
and Sobolev bounds

We define the norms of
L^{2}
-based weighted and Sobolev spaces as

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=\|\langle r\rangle\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)},

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}+\|\partial_{r}\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}+\|\frac{1}{r}\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)},

\displaystyle\|\zeta\|_{L^{2,1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}=\|\langle\xi\rangle\phi\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)},

\displaystyle\|\zeta\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}=\|\zeta\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}+\|\partial_{\xi}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}.

Proposition 5.3

(Boundedness of the Fourier transform on weighted
L^{2}
and Sobolev spaces)
.

The application
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
is bounded between the following spaces:

\displaystyle L^{2,1}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{1}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)

\displaystyle H^{1}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow L^{2,1}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)

\displaystyle L^{2,1}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}H^{1}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)

\displaystyle H^{1}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}L^{2,1}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi).

The application
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}
is bounded between the following spaces

\displaystyle H^{1}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow L^{2,1}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)

\displaystyle L^{2,1}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{1}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)

\displaystyle|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}L^{2}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow L^{2}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)

\displaystyle|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}L^{2,1}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{1}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r).

Proof.

We will follow closely the proof of Proposition
5.2
and we overtake its notations.

Step 1: Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}
from
H^{1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
L^{2,1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
and of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\sharp}
from
L^{2,1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
H^{1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
.

Pick
\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
. Since
\mathcal{H}\psi_{\sharp}(\xi,r)=\lambda(\xi)\psi_{\sharp}(\xi,r)
and
\sigma_{3}\mathcal{H}\sigma_{3}=\mathcal{H}^{*}
, we have that
\lambda(\xi)\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}\phi(\xi)=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}(\mathcal{H}\phi)(\xi)
. Since
\|\mathcal{H}\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{H^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
(where
\|\phi\|_{H^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}+\|\Delta_{1}\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
) and we have proved that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}
is continuous from
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
, and since
|\lambda(\xi)|\approx|\xi|^{2}
for
|\xi|>1
, we deduce that
\|\langle\xi\rangle^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}\phi\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{H^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
. Interpolating between this bound and the
L^{2}
bound, this implies
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}(\phi)\|_{L^{2,1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
.

Conversely, pick
\zeta\in\mathcal{S}
. Then we have similarly that
\mathcal{H}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\sharp}\zeta=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\sharp}(\lambda(\xi)\zeta)
. Hence as we have proved that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}^{-1}
is continuous from
L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
, and since
|\lambda(\xi)|\approx|\xi|^{2}
for
|\xi|\geq 1
, we have
\|\mathcal{H}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\sharp}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\|\langle\xi\rangle^{2}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}
. Using
\|u\|_{H^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\|\mathcal{H}u\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
we infer that
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\sharp}\zeta\|_{H^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\|\langle\xi\rangle^{2}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}
. Interpolating between this bound and the
L^{2}
bound, this implies
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}^{-1}\zeta\|_{H^{1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\|\zeta\|_{L^{2,1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}
.

Step 2: Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}
from
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
and of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\sharp}
from
H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}^{S}
from
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
and of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1S}_{\sharp}
from
H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
.

Pick
\phi\in L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. We consider the three terms in the right-hand side of (
5.7
). For the first one, since
\xi\mapsto\int v(r)J_{1}(\xi r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
is the two-dimensional Fourier transform of
x\mapsto e^{i\theta}v(r)
, the
L^{2,1}\rightarrow H^{1}
continuity of the Fourier transform and (
1.12
) imply

\left\|\widetilde{a}_{\sharp}(\xi)(1-\chi(\xi))\int_{0}^{\infty}e_{1}\cdot\phi(r)\chi(r)J_{1}(\xi r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}.

The two other terms in (
5.7
) can be bounded similarly by appealing to the
L^{2,1}\rightarrow H^{1}
continuity of the one-dimensional Fourier transform. Hence
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}\phi\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
.

The continuity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}_{\sharp}
from
H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
can be proved the same way, using the formula (
5.8
) and appealing to the
H^{1}\to L^{2,1}
continuity of the Fourier transform in one and two dimensions.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}^{R}
from
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
and of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\sharp}
from
H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
.

Pick
\phi\in L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
. We have by (
1.11
):

\partial_{\xi}\langle\psi^{R}_{\sharp}(\xi,r),\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle=\left\langle-\frac{r}{\langle r\rangle}m_{\sharp,1}^{R}(\xi,r)\sin(\xi r)+\frac{1}{\langle r\rangle}\partial_{\xi}(m_{\sharp,1}^{R})(\xi,r)\cos(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\langle r\rangle\phi\right\rangle+\mbox{similar terms}

Using that
|m_{\sharp,1}^{R}|+|\xi||\partial_{\xi}m_{\sharp,1}^{R}|\lesssim\frac{1}{|\xi|\langle r\rangle\langle\xi r\rangle^{1/2}}
and
\|\langle r\rangle\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
we have by Cauchy-Schwarz

\left|\langle-\frac{r}{\langle r\rangle}m_{\sharp,1}^{R}(\xi,r)\sin(\xi r)+\frac{1}{\langle r\rangle}\partial_{\xi}(m_{\sharp,1}^{R})(\xi,r)\cos(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\langle r\rangle\phi\rangle\right|\lesssim|\xi|^{-3/2}

for
|\xi|\geq 1/2
. This implies
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\sharp}^{R}\phi\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
.

Conversely, pick
\zeta\in H^{1}(|\xi|d\xi)
. Then, using (
1.11
) and
r\cos(\xi r)=\partial_{\xi}(\sin(\xi r))
, then integrating by parts in
\xi
we have:

\displaystyle r\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\sharp}\zeta(r)=-\langle\partial_{\xi}\zeta,(1-\chi(\xi))m^{R}_{\sharp,1}\lambda^{\prime}\sin(\xi r)\operatorname{sign}\xi\rangle_{L^{2}(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi}{\pi})}+\langle\zeta,\partial_{\xi}[](1-\chi(\xi))m^{R}_{\sharp,1}\lambda^{\prime}]\sin(\xi r)\operatorname{sign}\xi\rangle_{L^{2}(\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi}{\pi})}

\displaystyle+\mbox{similar}

Both terms above can be bounded exactly as in (
5.10
) using (
1.13
), so that
\|r\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\sharp}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
as desired.

Step 3: Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{S}_{\flat}
from
L^{2,1}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}H^{1}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
and from
L^{2,1}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
H^{1}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
,

and of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1S}_{\flat}
from
H^{1}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
and from
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}H^{1}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}
from
L^{2,1}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}H^{1}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
. Pick
\phi=(\varphi,-\varphi)^{\top}
with
\|\varphi\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=1
. We recall that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}\phi_{o}(\xi)
is given by the formulas (
5.11
)-(
5.12
).

For the contribution of
I
, we have that
\xi\mapsto\widetilde{b}_{\flat}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}
is a
C^{1}
function thanks to (
1.5
). By the
L^{2,1}\to H^{1}
continuity of the two dimensional Fourier transform, we obtain that

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}I(\xi,r)\varphi(r)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{H^{1}{(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}}\lesssim 1.

The contribution of
II
can be treated similarly, using the
L^{2,1}\to H^{1}
continuity of the one dimensional Fourier transform and that
1-\chi(r)
is supported on
\{r>1\}
, and we get

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}II(\xi,r)\varphi(r)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1.

For the contribution of
III
, we have

\partial_{\xi}III=\partial_{\xi}\left(\widetilde{b}_{\flat}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)\right)(\rho(r)-1)\chi(\xi r)+\widetilde{b}_{\flat}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)(\rho(r)-1)r\chi^{\prime}(\xi r)

so by (
1.5
) and
|\rho(r)-1|\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-2}
we get
|\partial_{\xi}III|\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-1}
. Hence

\left|\partial_{\xi}\int_{0}^{\infty}III(\xi,r)\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right|\lesssim\|\langle r\rangle^{-2}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\|\langle r\rangle\varphi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1

by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Hence
\|\int_{0}^{\infty}III(\xi,r)\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
. For the contribution of
IV
, we compute:

\displaystyle\partial_{\xi}IV

\displaystyle=\partial_{\xi}\left(\frac{c_{\flat}}{\sqrt{\xi}}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)\right)\sin(\xi r-\frac{\pi}{4})\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\left(\chi-\chi_{\xi^{-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{c_{\flat}}{\sqrt{\xi}}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)\cos(\xi r-\frac{\pi}{4})\sqrt{r}\left(\chi-\chi_{\xi^{-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{c_{\flat}}{\sqrt{\xi}}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)\cos(\xi r-\frac{\pi}{4})\sqrt{r}\chi^{\prime}_{\xi^{-1}}

Using
|\frac{c_{\flat}}{\sqrt{\xi}}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}|+|\xi||\partial_{\xi}\left(\frac{c_{\flat}}{\sqrt{\xi}}\langle\xi\rangle\frac{e_{1}(\xi)+e_{2}(\xi)}{\xi}\chi(\xi)\right)|\lesssim\sqrt{\xi}
we obtain that

\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\partial_{\xi}IV(\xi,r)\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right|\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\left\|\frac{1}{\langle r\rangle}\partial_{\xi}IV(\xi,r)\right\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim|\xi|^{-1/2}

by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Hence
\|\int_{0}^{\infty}IV(\xi,r)\varphi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
. This shows

\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}\phi_{o}\|_{\xi\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}
from
L^{2,1}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
H^{1}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
. Pick
\phi\in L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=1
(not necessarily even). We have that
\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}\phi(\xi)=\langle\partial_{\xi}(\chi(\xi)\psi^{S}_{\flat}(\xi,\cdot)),\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}
. For
\xi>0
,
\chi(\xi)\psi^{S}_{\flat}(\xi,\cdot)
can be decomposed as in (
5.13
) (and similarly for
\xi<0
using
\psi(-\xi)=-\sigma_{1}\psi(\xi)
). The contributions of
\partial_{\xi}I
,
\partial_{\xi}II
,
\partial_{\xi}III
and
\partial_{\xi}IV
can be treated exactly as the contributions of the corresponding terms in the above proof of the continuity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}
from
L^{2,1}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}H^{1}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
, and we obtain
\left\|\langle I+II+III+IV,\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\right\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
. Hence
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{S}\phi\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
as desired.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}
from
H^{1}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. Pick an even function of the form
\zeta(\xi)=\varphi(|\xi|)
with
\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}((0,\infty),|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}=1
. We recall that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}\zeta_{e}(r)=\int_{0}^{\infty}m^{-1S}_{\flat}(\xi,r)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi
where
m_{\flat}^{-1S}
is given by (
5.14
).

In the terms
I
and
II
, we have that
\xi\mapsto\frac{e(\xi)-e(-\xi)}{\xi}
,
\lambda^{\prime}
,
\widetilde{b}_{\flat}
and
c_{\flat}
are
C^{1}
with
c_{\flat}(0)=0
, thanks to (
1.5
). Hence, appealing to the
H^{1}\to L^{2,1}
continuity of the inverse Fourier transform in two and one dimensions, using that
1-\chi
has support in
\{r\geq 1\}
, we obtain

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}(I(\xi,r)+II(\xi,r))\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1.

For the third term we have, using
|\rho(r)-1|\lesssim r^{-2}
, for
r>1

\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}III(\xi,r)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right|\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\|\chi(\xi)\chi(\xi r)\|_{L^{2}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{3}}.

Hence,
\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}III(\xi,r)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
. Finally, for the fourth term we have, using the local
H^{1}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)\to L^{p}
embedding for
p>2
large and
|c_{\flat}|\lesssim|\xi|
, for
r>1
,

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}IV(\xi,r)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right\|_{L^{2}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{L^{p}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r}}\|\sqrt{\xi}\chi(\xi)\chi(\xi r)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{1+\frac{2}{p^{\prime}}}}

where
p^{\prime}
stands for the conjugate Lebesgue exponent of
p
. Hence
\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}IV(\xi,r)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
if one chooses
p^{\prime}
close enough to
1
.

Combining these estimates, we obtain
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}\zeta_{o}\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
as desired.

\bullet
Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1S}_{\flat}
from
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}H^{1}_{o}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}_{o}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. Pick a function
\zeta(\xi)=|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\varphi(\xi)
(not necessarily odd) with
\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}=1
. We recall that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}\zeta(r)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi_{\flat}^{S}(\xi,r)\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\chi(\xi)\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi
. We decompose
\chi(\xi)\psi_{\flat}^{S}
for
\xi>0
according to (
5.13
), and for
\xi<0
we also use the same decomposition since
\psi^{S}_{\flat}(-\xi)=-\sigma_{1}\psi^{S}_{\flat}(\xi)
. The terms
I
,
II
,
III
and
IV
can be treated exactly as the terms
I
,
II
,
III
and
IV
in (
5.14
) were treated above in the proof of the boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}
from
H^{1}_{e}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}_{e}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
, so that
\|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(I+II+III+IV)(\xi,r)\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}\varphi(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
. This shows
\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\flat}^{-1S}\zeta\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
.

Step 3: Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{R}_{\flat}
from
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
.
Picking
\phi\in L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
with
\|\phi\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=1
and differentiating the first term in (
5.15
) gives

\partial_{\xi}\left\langle\frac{m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}}{\xi}\chi(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=\left\langle\partial_{\xi}\left(\frac{m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}}{\xi}\right)\chi(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}+\left\langle\frac{m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}}{\xi}\chi^{\prime}(\xi r),\sigma_{3}r\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}.

For the first term using (
1.7
) and
\|\phi\|_{L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
we have
|\langle\partial_{\xi}(\xi^{-1}m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}})\chi(\xi r),\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}|\lesssim 1
. The second term can be bounded exactly as the first term in (
5.15
) was bounded above since
\|r\phi\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|\phi\|_{L^{2,1}}\lesssim 1
. Hence
\left\|\partial_{\xi}\left\langle\frac{m_{\flat}^{\operatorname{loc}}}{\xi}\chi(\xi r)),\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\right\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
.

We differentiate the second term in (
5.15
):

\partial_{\xi}\left\langle\frac{m_{\flat,1}^{R}}{\xi}\cos(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}=\left\langle\partial_{\xi}\left(\frac{m_{\flat,1}^{R}}{\xi}\right)\cos(\xi r)-r\frac{m_{\flat,1}^{R}}{\xi}\sin(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\phi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}

We have
\|\langle r\rangle^{-1}|\xi|^{-1}m_{\flat,1}^{R}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1+\ln^{2}|\xi|
and
\|r\frac{m_{\flat,1}^{R}}{\xi}\sin(\xi r)\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1+\ln^{2}|\xi|
by (
1.8
). Using
\|r\phi\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
and Cauchy-Schwarz this implies
|\partial_{\xi}\langle\xi^{-1}m_{\flat,1}^{R}\cos(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}|\lesssim 1+\ln^{2}|\xi|
so we deduce that
\|\partial_{\xi}\langle\xi^{-1}m_{\flat,1}^{R}\cos(\xi r),\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1

Combining with the previous estimates for the two terms in (
5.15
), we get
\|\frac{1}{\xi}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{R}_{\flat}\phi\|_{H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
.

Step 4: Boundedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\flat}
from
H^{1}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
.
In the decomposition (
5.16
), we already know the first term defines a bounded application from
L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
to
L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
thanks to (
5.17
). Hence it suffices to bound the second one.

To do so, we integrate by parts using
r\cos(\xi r)=\partial_{\xi}\sin(\xi r)
and obtain

\displaystyle r\left\langle\zeta,\chi(\xi)m_{\flat,1}^{R}\cos(\xi r)\lambda^{\prime}\operatorname{sign}\xi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\partial_{\xi}(\chi(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}\zeta),m_{\flat,1}^{R}\sin(\xi r)\operatorname{sign}\xi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}-\left\langle\chi(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}\zeta,\partial_{\xi}m_{\flat,1}^{R}\sin(\xi r)\operatorname{sign}\xi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\partial_{\xi}(\chi(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}\zeta),[-m_{\flat,1}^{R}\sin(\xi r)+\int_{0}^{\xi}(\partial_{\xi}m_{\flat,1}^{R}(r,\eta)\sin(\eta r)d\eta)]\operatorname{sign}\xi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}.

The term above is estimated exactly as the first term in (
5.16
) was estimated above, using that
\|\partial_{\xi}(\chi(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}\zeta)\|_{L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim 1
, and that
|\int_{0}^{\xi}(\partial_{\xi}m_{\flat,1}^{R}(r,\eta)\sin(\eta r)d\eta)|\lesssim\frac{\xi^{1/2}\ln^{2}(\xi)}{\langle r\rangle^{3/2}}
from (
1.8
), producing

\left\|\left\langle\partial_{\xi}(\chi(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}\zeta),[-m_{\flat,1}^{R}\sin(\xi r)+\int_{0}^{\xi}(\partial_{\xi}m_{\flat,1}^{R}(r,\eta)\sin(\eta r)d\eta)]\operatorname{sign}\xi\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\right\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1.

Injecting this estimate and (
5.17
) in (
5.16
), we deduce
\|r\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1R}_{\flat}\zeta\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
as desired.
∎

We finish by a straightforward adaptation of the previous proposition to some higher order regularity which is a technical result that will be useful later on.

Lemma 5.3
.

For all
s\in(1,3)
, the distorted Fourier transform
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
is bounded from
L^{2,s}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
H^{s}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Proposition
5.2
, and is actually simpler since it does not require to track cancellations near the origin for
\widetilde{\phi}_{o}
. The limitation
s\in(1,3)
comes to the
\xi^{2}\log^{2}\xi
singularity in the estimates of Theorem
1.4
, because
\xi\mapsto\xi^{2}\log^{2}\xi\in H^{s}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
for such
s
but this function does not belong to
H^{3}
.
∎

6.
Bijectivity of the distorted Fourier transform

6.1.
The main result

Recall that the distorted Fourier transform and its inverse were defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\phi)(\xi)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\phi(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\zeta)(r)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\zeta(\xi)\psi(\xi,r)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

In Section
5
we proved various bounds enjoyed by these transformations. We now turn to our main theorem on the distorted Fourier transform: it asserts that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}
are indeed inverses of each other, and that they can be used to define the evolution group.

Theorem 6.1
.

(i)

(Right inverse)
There holds

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}=\operatorname{Id}\qquad\mbox{on $\widetilde{L^{2}}$}

(ii)

(Left inverse)

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\operatorname{Id}\qquad\mbox{on $L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)$}.

(iii)

(Plancherel identity)
For
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
,

\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}\sigma_{3}\phi_{1}(r)\cdot\phi_{2}(r)\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\widetilde{\phi_{1}}(\xi)\widetilde{\phi_{2}}(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

(iv)

(Diagonalization of
\mathcal{H}
) The distorted Fourier transform conjugates multiplication by
\lambda
to the operator
\mathcal{H}
: if
\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
,

\mathcal{H}\phi=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\lambda\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\phi.

(v)

(Evolution group) If
\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

e^{it\mathcal{H}}\phi(r)=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}e^{it\lambda}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\phi(r)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{it\lambda(\xi)}\widetilde{\phi}(\xi)\psi(\xi,r)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi.

Proof.

Items
(i)
and
(ii)
will be the object of subsections
6.2
and
6.3
below. Taking these two assertions for granted, the rest of the theorem follows.

Indeed, assuming
(i)
, we can write
\phi_{2}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{\phi_{2}}(\xi)
; inserting this identity in the expression
\int\sigma\phi_{1}(r)\cdot\phi_{2}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
and applying Fubini’s theorem gives Plancherel’s identity
(iii)
.

Similarly, we have for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
that

\mathcal{H}\phi=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{H}\phi)
. An integration by parts using (
3.1
) shows
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{H}\phi)(\xi)=\langle\mathcal{H}\psi(\xi,\cdot),\sigma_{3}\phi\rangle=\lambda(\xi)\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\phi(\xi)
, and this gives the diagonalization formula
(iv)
.
The evolution group formula
(v)
then follows directly from
(iv)
upon time integration.

Remark 6.2
.

Our proof of (ii) bypasses the rigorous justification of the limit in which it was formally obtained from Stone’s formula in Section
4.1
. The approach we developp is general: our proof shows that this identity holds true except on a subspace spanned by eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{H}
(which in the present case do not exist, so we conclude the validity of this identity). We believe this approach to be applicable to other equations, up to dealing the projection on the discrete spectrum.

We mention that Chen and Luhrmann
[
3
]
faced a similar problem of justifying (v) for the linearization of the sine-Gordon equation around a moving kink, but they could use a connectedness argument specific to this equation.

6.2.
Proof of the right inverse

We prove here that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}=\operatorname{Id}
on
\widetilde{L^{2}}
. By the
L^{2}
boundedness result in Proposition
5.2
, it suffices to prove this identity on
\mathcal{S}

Pick
\zeta\in\mathcal{S}
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. We assume without loss of generality that
\zeta
is real-valued, and that
\xi>0
. We assume first
\xi\geq 1
so that by (
1.9
) and
b_{\sharp}^{2}(\xi)+c_{\sharp}^{2}(\xi)=1
, we have

(6.1)

\displaystyle\psi(\xi,r)

\displaystyle=\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\xi,r)+\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\xi,r)=\frac{d(\xi)e^{i\xi r}+\bar{d}(\xi)e^{-i\xi r}}{\sqrt{\xi r}}e(\xi)+\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,1}^{R}(r,\xi)e^{i\xi r}+\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,2}^{R}e^{-i\xi r}

(6.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{d(\xi)e^{i\xi r}+\bar{d}(\xi)e^{-i\xi r}}{\sqrt{\xi r}}e(\xi)+O\left(r^{-\frac{3}{2}}\right)

where
d(\xi)=(\frac{b_{\sharp}(\xi)}{2}+\frac{c_{\sharp}(\xi)}{2i})e^{-i\frac{3\pi}{4}}
satisfies
|d|^{2}=1/4
, and where
\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,1}^{R}=\frac{1}{2}(m_{\sharp,1}^{R}-im_{\sharp,1}^{R})
and
\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,1}^{R}=\frac{1}{2}(m_{\sharp,1}^{R}+im_{\sharp,1}^{R})
. We have
|\psi(\xi,r)|\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-1/2}
using (
6.2
), and we have
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\zeta\in L^{2,1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
by Proposition
5.2
, so that
\psi(\xi,\cdot)\cdot\sigma_{3}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\zeta\in L^{1}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. Therefore, by Fubini,

(6.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\zeta(\xi)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\zeta(\eta)\lambda^{\prime}(\eta)\operatorname{sign}(\eta)\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi(\eta,r)\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

Now for fixed numbers
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\eta\neq\xi
and
R>1
, by (
3.1
) we have

\displaystyle(\lambda(\xi)-\lambda(\eta))\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{r}{R})\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi(\eta,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{r}{R})\mathcal{H}\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi(\eta,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r-\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{r}{R})\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\mathcal{H}^{*}(\sigma_{3}\psi(\eta,r))r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\chi(\frac{r}{R})\mathcal{H}\psi(\xi,r)-\mathcal{H}\left(\chi(\frac{r}{R})\psi(\xi,r)\right)\right)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi(\eta,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(R^{-2}\chi^{\prime\prime}(\frac{r}{R})-r^{-1}R^{-1}\chi^{\prime}(\frac{r}{R})\right)\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\psi(\eta,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r+2R^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi^{\prime}(\frac{r}{R})\partial_{r}\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\psi(\eta,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=I(\eta,R)+II(\eta,R).

We have
I(\eta,R)=O(R^{-1/2})
uniformly in
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
, using
|\psi(\xi,r)|\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-1/2}
and
|\psi(\eta,r)|\lesssim 1
(this second inequality being uniform in
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and
r\in(0,\infty)
). For the second term, on the one hand for any
\kappa>0
if
|\eta|>\kappa
then using
|\psi(\eta,r)|\lesssim_{\kappa}\langle r\rangle^{-1/2}
we see it is
II(\eta,R)=O_{\kappa}(1)
. On the other hand for any
\nu>0
, if
|\eta-\xi|>\nu
and
|\eta+\xi|>\nu
, assuming
\eta\geq 1
without loss of generality for the computation, we decompose according to (
6.2
) both
\psi(\xi,r)
and
\psi(\eta,r)
and obtain

\displaystyle II(\eta,R)

\displaystyle=R^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi^{\prime}(\frac{r}{R})\frac{i\xi d(\xi)e^{i\xi r}-i\xi\bar{d}(\xi)e^{-i\xi r}}{\sqrt{\xi r}}\frac{d(\eta)e^{i\eta r}+\bar{d}(\eta)e^{-i\eta r}}{\sqrt{\eta r}}e(\xi)\cdot e(\eta)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r+O(R^{-1})

\displaystyle=O_{\nu}(R^{-1})

upon integrating by parts using a non-stationary phase argument since
\eta\notin\{-\xi,\xi\}
. The same estimate can be obtained for
\eta\leq-1
using
\psi(\eta)=-\sigma_{1}\psi(-\eta)
and the above computations, and for
|\eta|\leq 1
using the decomposition (
1.4
) and (
1.6
) and a non-stationary phase argument again. Moreover, one verifies easily that the constant in the
O_{\nu}(R^{-1})
does indeed solely depend on
\nu
. Combining these observations, we have for any
\nu>0
small that

(6.4)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{r}{R})\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi(\eta,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l l}O(1)\qquad\mbox{for }\eta\in\{|\eta+\xi|\leq\nu\},\\
O_{\nu}(R^{-1})\qquad\mbox{for }\eta\in\{|\eta-\xi|>\nu\}\cap\{|\eta+\xi|>\nu\}.\end{array}\right.

In particular, on the set
\{|\eta-\xi|>\nu\}
, the function
\eta\mapsto\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{r}{R})\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi(\eta,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
is bounded uniformly for
R>1
, and converges to
0
as
R\to\infty
at everypoint except
\eta=-\xi
. Taking
\nu>0
small enough and appealing to the dominated convergence theorem as
R\to\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\zeta(\xi)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{\eta-\xi}{\nu})\zeta(\eta)\lambda^{\prime}(\eta)\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\psi(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi(\eta,r)\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

We now fix
\nu>0
small for the moment, and will take the limit
\nu\to 0
later on. By the decomposition (
1.9
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\zeta(\xi)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{\eta-\xi}{\nu})\zeta(\eta)\lambda^{\prime}(\eta)\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\Bigg(\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\eta,r)

(6.5)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\eta,r)+\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\eta,r)+\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\eta,r)\Bigg)\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\Bigg)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

Using
|\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\xi,r)|\lesssim\langle r\rangle^{-3/2}
, the last term in the inner integral of (
6.5
) is bounded by

(6.6)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(r,\eta)\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=O(1)

uniformly for
R>1
. By (
6.1
), the second term is

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(r,\eta)\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=O(1)+\int_{2}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\frac{d(\xi)e^{i\xi r}+\bar{d}(\xi)e^{-i\xi r}}{\sqrt{\xi r}}e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}\left(\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,1}^{R}(r,\eta)e^{i\eta r}+\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,2}^{R}(\eta,r)e^{-i\eta r}\right)\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r.

The integral can be decomposed into terms of the form

\displaystyle\int_{2}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)e^{i(\xi-\eta)r}e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,1}^{R}(\eta,r)\sqrt{r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\int_{2}^{\infty}\chi(|\xi-\eta|r)\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)e^{i(\xi-\eta)r}e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,1}^{R}(\eta,r)\sqrt{r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\int_{2}^{\infty}(1-\chi(|\xi-\eta|r))\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)e^{i(\xi-\eta)r}e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,1}^{R}(\eta,r)\sqrt{r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

and of similar contributions with
e^{i(\xi+\eta)r}
,
e^{i(\eta-\xi)r}
,
e^{-i(\xi+\eta)r}
and
\widetilde{m}_{\sharp,2}^{R}(\eta,r)
. In order to bound the first term in the right-hand side above we simply use
|m_{\sharp,1}^{R}(\eta,r)|\lesssim r^{-3/2}
and see it is
O(|\ln|\xi-\eta||)
. The second term in the right-hand side can be bounded using
e^{i(\xi-\eta)r}=-i(\xi-\eta)^{-1}\partial_{r}(e^{i(\xi-\eta)r})
, then integrating by parts and using
|\partial_{r}^{j}m_{\sharp,1}^{R}(\eta,r)|\lesssim r^{-3/2-j}
for
j=0,1
, and we obtain that it is
O(1)
. The other contributions can be estimated similarly, and therefore, if
|\xi-\eta|\lesssim\nu
,

(6.7)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{R}(r,\eta)\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=O(|\ln|\xi-\eta||).

The third term in (
6.5
) can be estimated as above, which, injected in (
6.5
) with (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) shows

(6.8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\zeta(\xi)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{\eta-\xi}{\nu})\zeta(\eta)\lambda^{\prime}(\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\eta,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r+O(\nu|\ln\nu|)

We now inject (
6.1
) in the above formula and compute

(6.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{\eta-\xi}{\nu})\zeta(\eta)\lambda^{\prime}(\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\xi,r)\cdot\sigma_{3}\psi_{\sharp}^{S}(\eta,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Re}\Bigg[\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{\eta-\xi}{\nu})\zeta(\eta)\lambda^{\prime}(\eta)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi\eta}}e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}e(\eta)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)\left(d(\xi)d(\eta)e^{i(\xi+\eta)r}+d(\xi)\overline{d(\eta)}e^{i(\xi-\eta)r}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\Bigg]+O(\nu),

where we used that
\zeta
is real-valued. For the first term if
|\xi-\eta|<2\nu
then
|\xi+\eta|>|\xi|
for
\nu
small enough, so that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)d(\xi)d(\eta)e^{i(\xi+\eta)r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=O(1)
by a non-stationary phase argument, implying

(6.10)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{\eta-\xi}{\nu})\zeta(\eta)\lambda^{\prime}(\eta)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi\eta}}e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}e(\eta)\mathfrak{Re}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)d(\xi)d(\eta)e^{i(\xi+\eta)r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta=O(\nu)

uniformly for
R>1
. We then explicitly compute the second term, recognizing the Fourier transform of the Heaviside function,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{\eta-\xi}{\nu})\zeta(\eta)\lambda^{\prime}(\eta)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi\eta}}e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}e(\eta)\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi\left(\frac{r}{R}\right)d(\xi)\overline{d(\eta)}e^{i(\xi-\eta)r}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle=4\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi(\frac{\eta-\xi}{\nu})\zeta(\eta)\lambda^{\prime}(\eta)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi\eta}}e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}e(\eta)d(\xi)\overline{d(\eta)}\left(\frac{1}{2}\delta(\xi-\eta)-\frac{i}{\pi}p.v.\frac{1}{\eta-\xi}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

(6.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\xi}\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}e(\xi)|d(\xi)|^{2}\zeta(\xi)+O(\nu)\ =\zeta(\xi)+O(\nu)

where we used
|d(\xi)|^{2}=1/4
and
\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)e(\xi)\cdot\sigma_{3}e(\xi)=2\xi
. Injecting (
6.10
), (
6.11
) in (
6.9
) and then in (
6.9
) shows

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\zeta(\xi)=\zeta(\xi)+O(\nu|\log\nu|)=\zeta(\xi)\quad\mbox{as $\nu\to 0$}

This is the desired result in the case
\xi>2
.

In the case
0<\xi\leq 1/2
, we remark that between the items (i) and (ii) of Theorem
1.4
, the validity of (ii) for
\xi\geq\frac{1}{2}
was arbitrary. It is clear from the proof that for any
\delta>0
the decomposition (
1.9
) is also valid for any
|\xi|>\delta
, up to changing the value of all constants in the estimates in (ii) depending on
\delta
. Hence by taking
\delta
small we see the identities (
6.1
)-(
6.2
) are also valid for
0<\xi\leq 1
, so that the same proof as for
\xi\geq 1
actually applies, and we get
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\zeta(\xi)=\zeta(\xi)
in that case as well. The equality for
\xi=0
is then a consequence of Lemma
5.1
.(i).
∎

6.3.
Proof of the right inverse

We prove here that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\operatorname{Id}
on
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. We already know that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}=\operatorname{Id}
on
\widetilde{L^{2}}
. This implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
is surjective, hence

\textrm{Im}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})=\widetilde{L^{2}}.

Step 1.
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\operatorname{Id}
if and only if
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}
is surjective
from
\widetilde{L^{2}}
onto
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. Indeed, if
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}
is surjective, then any
u\in L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
can be written as
u=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\zeta
for some
\zeta\in\widetilde{L^{2}}
. Then

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(u)=u\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\underbrace{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}}_{\displaystyle\mathrm{Id}}(\zeta)=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\zeta),

which proves the desired property.

Step 2. Closedness of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{L^{2}})
.
Indeed, taking
u_{n}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{L^{2}})
a sequence converging in
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
, there exists

\varphi_{n}\in\widetilde{L^{2}}
such that
u_{n}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\varphi_{n})
hence

\varphi_{n}=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(u_{n})

thanks to the identity
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}=\operatorname{Id}
. By continuity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
(see Lemma

5.2
), we deduce that
(\varphi_{n})_{e}
and
(\varphi_{n})_{o}
converge respectively in
L^{2}(|\xi|d\xi)
and
|\xi|\langle\xi\rangle^{-1}L^{2}(|\xi|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
: let
\varphi_{\infty}\in\textrm{Im}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
be such that the limits are
(\varphi_{\infty})_{e}
and
(\varphi_{\infty})_{o}
respectively. By continuity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}
,

u_{n}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\varphi_{\infty})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{L^{2}}),

concluding the proof that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{L^{2}})
is closed.

We now define
E=(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{L^{2}}))^{\bot}
. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{L^{2}})
is closed, we have
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)=E+\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{L^{2}})
. Our goal is to show that
E=\{0\}
.

Step 3. Characterization of the orthogonal
E
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{L^{2}})
.
We claim that

E=\left\{u\in L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r),\,\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}u(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{\widetilde{L^{2}}}=0\right\}.

Here, note that
\int_{0}^{\infty}u(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{3}u)
is well-defined as a function in
\widetilde{L^{2}}
by boundedness of
\widetilde{F}
(Proposition
5.2
).

In order to prove this characterization of
E
, we will rely on the Plancherel-like identity

(6.12)

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}u(r)\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\zeta(\xi)\psi(\xi,r)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}(\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\\
&\qquad\qquad=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}u(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right)\zeta(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}(\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\end{split}

or equivalently

(6.13)

\int_{0}^{\infty}u(r)\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\zeta)(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{3}u)(\xi)\zeta(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}(\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

which we claim holds for
u\in L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
and
\zeta\in\widetilde{L^{2}}
. Let us first check that the left- and right-hand sides in the above expressions make sense; this follows by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Proposition
5.2
:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}u(r)\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\zeta)(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right|\leq\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\left\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\zeta)\right\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{\widetilde{L^{2}}},

\displaystyle\left|\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{3}u)(\xi)\zeta(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}(\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\left|\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{3}u)_{o}(\xi)\zeta_{e}(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}(\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{3}u)_{e}(\xi)\zeta_{o}(\xi)\lambda^{\prime}(\xi)\operatorname{sign}(\xi)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\lesssim\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{3}u)_{o}(\xi)\right|\left|\zeta_{e}(\xi)\right|\langle\xi\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi+\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\left|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{3}u)_{e}(\xi)\right|\left|\zeta_{o}(\xi)\right|\langle\xi\rangle\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\lesssim\|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{3}u)\|_{\widetilde{L^{2}}}\|\zeta\|_{\widetilde{L^{2}}}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\left\|\zeta\right\|_{\widetilde{L^{2}}}.

This proves that the integrals appearing in (
6.12
) and (
6.13
) are well-defined. Furthermore, these equalities hold by Fubini’s theorem if
u
and
\zeta
are integrable. By a density argument and the above bounds, we conclude that (
6.12
) and (
6.13
) hold for all
u\in L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
and
\zeta\in\widetilde{L^{2}}
.

In light of (
6.12
) and the above bounds, we see that
u
is orthogonal to
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}(\widetilde{L^{2}})
(i.e.
u\in E
) if and only if
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(\sigma_{3}u)=0
, which is the desired characterization of
E
.

Step 4.
E=\{0\}
or
\dim E=+\infty
.
For any
u\in L^{2}
and
\lambda_{0}\in i\mathbb{R}^{\ast}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}((\mathcal{H}-\lambda_{0})^{-1})^{\ast}(u)(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}u(r)\cdot(\mathcal{H}-\lambda_{0})^{-1}(\psi(\xi,.))(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda(\xi)-\lambda_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}u(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

(these equalities hold in
\widetilde{L^{2}}
; in order to show them rigorously, we start with
u
smooth and decaying and argue by density). In particular, if
u\in E
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}((\mathcal{H}-\lambda_{0})^{-1})^{\ast}(u)(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\frac{1}{\lambda(\xi)-\lambda_{0}}\int_{0}^{\infty}u(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=0.

This shows that
((\mathcal{H}-\lambda_{0})^{-1})^{\ast}E\subset E
. We can now argue by contradiction: if
E
was finite-dimensional and not reduced to
\{0\}
, then
((\mathcal{H}-\lambda_{0})^{-1})_{|E}^{\ast}
would have a nonzero eigenvector by the Jordan normal form theorem. In other words, this gives the existence of
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
and
u\in L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)\setminus\{0\}
such that

(\mathcal{H}^{\ast}-\lambda_{0})^{-1}u=\mu u.

This implies that
u
belongs to the domain of
\mathcal{H}^{\ast}-\lambda_{0}
. Composing the above equality by
\mathcal{H}^{\ast}-\lambda_{0}
, we obtain

u=\mu(\mathcal{H}^{\ast}-\lambda_{0})u.

We learn first from this identity that
\mu\neq 0
and second, since
\mathcal{H}^{\ast}=\sigma_{3}\mathcal{H}\sigma_{3}
, that
\sigma_{3}u
is a nonzero eigenvector of
\mathcal{H}
. Since
\mathcal{H}
does not have nonzero eigenvectors by Theorem
1.3
, this provides the sought contradiction.

Step 5. Compactness of the closed unit ball of
E
.
We learned from step 4 that it suffices to show that
\dim E<\infty
, which, by Riesz’ lemma, is equivalent to the compactness of the (closed) unit ball of
E
. We now assume that
(v_{n})
is a sequence of elements in the unit ball of
E
, and will show that it admits a convergent subsequence.

By weak compactness of the closed unit ball of
L^{2}
, it admits a weakly convergent subsequence which we also denote
(v_{n})
for simplicity:
v_{n}\overset{L^{2}}{\rightharpoonup}v_{\infty}
. Since
E
is weakly closed,
v_{\infty}\in E
and we can consider the sequence
u_{n}=v_{n}-v_{\infty}
. It is a sequence in
E
which is converging weakly to zero, and we will now show that it actually converges strongly.

Step 6: reformulating the orthogonality condition for
(u_{n})
. The sequence
(u_{n})
is such that

\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1,\qquad u_{n}\overset{n\to\infty}{\rightharpoonup}0,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}u_{n}(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{\widetilde{L^{2}}}=0.

We now want to replace the functions
\psi(\xi,r)
, which have a complicated structure, with more simple model functions. This can be achieved to the cost of replacing equality to zero by convergence to zero: we claim that there exists a function
\beta\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R})
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)u_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

The proof of this fact (and the properties of
\beta(\xi)
) will be delegated to Lemma
6.1
below.

Step 7. Strong convergence of
u_{n}
to
0
in
L^{2}
as
n\to\infty
.

We now prove that
u_{n}
converge strongly to
0
in
L^{2}
as
n\to\infty
. Recall that the two-dimensional radial Fourier transform is such that

\widehat{u}(\xi)=\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\xi r)u(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r,\qquad u(r)=\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\xi r)\widehat{u}(\xi)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

First, we claim that for any
u\in L^{2}
, if we define
\hat{v}=\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}\hat{u}\in L^{2}
, then we have the
identity

(6.14)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)u(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r-\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)v(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}-\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\right)K_{0,1}(\eta,\xi)\hat{u}(\eta)\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta,

where

K_{0,1}(\eta,\xi)=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\eta r)J_{1}(\xi r)e^{-\varepsilon r}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

which is studied in Lemma
6.2
. To do so, we first compute for

\varepsilon>0
and
v(r)=e^{-\varepsilon r}\widehat{\left(\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}\hat{u}\right)}
that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)u(r)e^{-\varepsilon r}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r-\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)v(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\eta r)\hat{u}(\eta)\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta e^{-\varepsilon r}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)e^{-\varepsilon r}\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\eta r)\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\hat{u}(\eta)\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}-\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\eta r)J_{1}(\xi r)e^{-\varepsilon r}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\hat{u}(\eta)\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

and we conclude by taking
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
. Applying
(
6.14
) to the sequence
u_{n}
, by its weak convergence to
0
and
Lemma
6.2
, we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}-\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\right)K_{0,1}(\eta,\xi)\widehat{u_{n}}(\eta)\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\rightarrow 0

strongly in
L^{2}
when
n\rightarrow+\infty
.

Using this result and Plancherel’s theorem,

(6.15)

\begin{split}0&\longleftarrow\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)u_{n}(r)+J_{1}(\xi r)v_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right)\right)^{2}\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\\
&\;\;=\underbrace{\left\|\frac{\widehat{u_{n}}}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}\right\|_{L^{2}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}^{2}+\|v_{n}\|_{L^{2}(r\operatorname{dr})}^{2}}_{\displaystyle I}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\underbrace{2\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)u_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)v_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi}_{\displaystyle II}\end{split}

On the one hand, by another application of Plancherel’s theorem,

(6.16)

I=\left\|\frac{\widehat{u_{n}}}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}\right\|_{L^{2}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}^{2}+\|v_{n}\|_{L^{2}(r\operatorname{dr})}^{2}\sim\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}^{2}.

On the other hand, since

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)v_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\eta r)\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\widehat{u_{n}}(\eta)\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta\,r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\widehat{u_{n}}(\eta)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)J_{0}(\eta r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right)\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

we can write

\displaystyle II=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)u_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)v_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{u_{n}}(\xi)\widehat{u_{n}}(\eta)K(\xi,\eta)\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\,\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{u_{n}}(\xi)\widehat{u_{n}}(\eta)(K(\xi,\eta)+K(\eta,\xi))\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi\,\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta

with

K(\xi,\eta)=\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}K_{0,1}(\eta,\xi),

We now claim that
K(\xi,\eta)+K(\eta,\xi)
is a Hilbert-Schmidt kernel. This will imply that

(6.17)

II\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,

and gathering (
6.15
), (
6.16
) and (
6.17
) this will give that
u_{n}\to 0
in
L^{2}
as
n\to\infty
.

In order to check that
K(\xi,\eta)+K(\eta,\xi)
is Hilbert-Schmidt, we rely on the following decomposition

\displaystyle K(\xi,\eta)+K(\eta,\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{|\xi-\eta|>1}\left[\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}K_{0,1}(\eta,\xi)+\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}K_{0,1}(\xi,\eta)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathbf{1}_{|\xi-\eta|<1}\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\left[K_{0,1}(\eta,\xi)+K_{0,1}(\xi,\eta)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathbf{1}_{|\xi-\eta|<1}\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\left[\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}-\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\right]K_{0,1}(\xi,\eta)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\mathbf{1}_{|\xi-\eta|<1}\frac{\beta(\eta)}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}\left[\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\eta)\rangle}-\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}\right]K_{0,1}(\xi,\eta)

The fact that this kernel is Hilbert-Schmidt now follows from Lemma
6.2
, more specifically the application of its third, fourth and second assertions to the first, second and third and fourth lines on the right-hand side, respectively.

This concludes the proof of the identity
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\operatorname{Id}
; below, we will prove lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
which were part of the argument.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists a function
\xi\rightarrow\beta(\xi)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R})
such that

\xi\rightarrow\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\xi},\beta^{\prime}(\xi)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})

with the following property: assume that the sequence
(u_{n})
is such that

\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1,\qquad u_{n}\overset{n\to\infty}{\rightharpoonup}0,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}u_{n}(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{\widetilde{L^{2}}}=0;

then

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)u_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Proof.

Step 1: Even and odd parts

By Step 3, the definition of
\widetilde{L^{2}}
and Lemma
5.2
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}u_{n}(r)\cdot\psi(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{\widetilde{L^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}u_{n,o}(r)\cdot\psi_{o}(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}+\left\|\frac{\langle\xi\rangle}{|\xi|}\int_{0}^{\infty}u_{n,e}(r)\cdot\psi_{e}(\xi,r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)},

where we recall the definitions

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\psi_{e}(\xi,r)=\frac{\psi(\xi,r)+\sigma_{1}\psi(\xi,r)}{2}\\
&\psi_{o}(\xi,r)=\frac{\psi(\xi,r)-\sigma_{1}\psi(\xi,r)}{2}\end{cases}\qquad\begin{cases}&u_{n,e}(r)=\frac{u_{n}(r)+\sigma_{1}u_{n}(r)}{2}\\
&u_{n,o}(r)=\frac{u_{n}(r)-\sigma_{1}u_{n}(r)}{2}.\end{cases}

Setting
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
as follows

u_{n,e}(r)=\begin{pmatrix}a_{n}(r)\\
a_{n}(r)\end{pmatrix}\qquad u_{n,o}(r)=\begin{pmatrix}b_{n}(r)\\
-b_{n}(r)\end{pmatrix},

they are such that

\|a_{n}\|_{L^{2}}+\|b_{n}\|_{L^{2}}\sim\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}

and the above identity can be written as

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}b_{n}(r)\left[\psi^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi^{2}(\xi,r)\right]r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left\|\frac{\langle\xi\rangle}{|\xi|}\int_{0}^{\infty}a_{n}(r)\left[\psi^{1}(\xi,r)+\psi^{2}(\xi,r)\right]r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}=0

We fix two real numbers
0<\xi_{0}<\xi_{1}<\infty
and we will prove different estimates in each of the three ranges
\xi\in[0,\xi_{0})
,
[\xi_{0},\xi_{1}]
,
[\xi_{1},\infty)
. We focus first on the case of
(b_{n})
and will explain at the end why the desired limit holds for
(a_{n})
.

Step 2: Medium frequencies and definition of
\beta(\xi)

By Theorem
1.4
we have that for any
0<\xi_{0}<\xi_{1}
and
\xi\in[\xi_{0},\xi_{1}]
,

\left|\psi(\xi,r)-\frac{\cos(\xi r-\alpha(\xi))}{\sqrt{\xi r}}e(\xi)\right|\lesssim_{\xi_{0},\xi_{1}}\frac{1}{\langle r\rangle^{3/2}},

where
\xi\rightarrow\alpha(\xi)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R})
with

\alpha(\xi)=\frac{\pi}{4}+O_{\xi\rightarrow 0}(\xi)
and
\alpha(\xi)=\frac{3\pi}{4}+O_{\xi\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\xi}\right)

as well as
\alpha^{\prime}(\xi)=O_{\xi\rightarrow 0}(1)
and
\alpha^{\prime}(\xi)=O_{\xi\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\right)
.

Since
J_{0}(r)=\frac{\cos\left(r-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}{\sqrt{r}}\left(1+O_{r\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\right)
and
J_{1}(r)=\frac{\cos\left(r-\frac{3\pi}{4}\right)}{\sqrt{r}}\left(1+O_{r\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\right)
, the function

\xi\rightarrow\beta(\xi)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R})
is defined as
the unique one such that

\psi(\xi,r)=\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)+O_{r\rightarrow+\infty}\left(\frac{1}{r^{3/2}}\right).

Its properties when
\xi\rightarrow 0
and
\xi\rightarrow+\infty
follows
from the ones of
\alpha(\xi)
. We decompose

\psi(\xi,r)=\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)e(\xi)+\psi_{\heartsuit}(\xi,r)

and we check that
|\psi_{\heartsuit}(\xi,r)|\lesssim_{\xi_{0},\xi_{1}}\frac{1}{\langle r\rangle^{3/2}}
. Denoting

d(\xi)=e_{1}(\xi)-e_{2}(\xi),\qquad d(\xi)\sim_{\xi_{0},\xi_{1}}1\;\mbox{if}\;\xi\in[\xi_{0},\xi_{1}],

we can write

\displaystyle d(\xi)\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r=\underbrace{\int_{0}^{\infty}(\psi^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi^{2}(\xi,r))b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}_{\displaystyle 0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\int_{0}^{\infty}(\psi_{\heartsuit}^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi_{\heartsuit}^{2}(\xi,r))b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r.

Since

\int_{\xi_{0}}^{\xi_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\psi_{\heartsuit}^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi_{\heartsuit}^{2}(\xi,r))^{2}rdr\xi d\xi<+\infty,

the operator

b\rightarrow\int_{0}^{\infty}(\psi_{\heartsuit}^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi_{\heartsuit}^{2}(\xi,r))b(r)rdr

is a Hilbert-Schmidt integral operator in
L^{2}([\xi_{0},\xi_{1}])
, and by weak convergence
of
b_{n}
in
L^{2}
, we deduce that

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}(\psi_{\heartsuit}^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi_{\heartsuit}^{2}(\xi,r))b_{n}(r)rdr\right\|_{L^{2}([\xi_{0},\xi_{1}])}\rightarrow 0

when
n\rightarrow+\infty
. We have therefore shown that for any
0<\xi_{0}<\xi_{1}
,

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)b_{n}(r)rdr\right\|_{L^{2}([\xi_{0},\xi_{1}])}\rightarrow 0

when
n\rightarrow+\infty.

Step 3: Low frequencies.

Before all, we need a precise description of
\psi(\xi,r)
at low frequencies. It is provided by Theorem
1.4
which gives the following decomposition

\psi(\xi,r)=c_{0}(\xi)J_{0}(r\xi)e(\xi)+\frac{c_{1}(\xi)\cos(\xi r)+c_{2}(\xi)\sin(\xi r)}{\langle\xi r\rangle^{1/2}}e(\xi)+\psi_{\diamondsuit}(\xi,r)

where
|c_{0}(\xi)-1|+|c_{1}(\xi)|+|c_{2}(\xi)|\lesssim|\xi|^{2}\ln^{2}(|\xi|)
and

\left|\psi_{\diamondsuit}(\xi,r)\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{(1+r)^{2}}+\frac{|\xi|^{2}\ln^{2}(|\xi|)}{\langle\xi r\rangle^{3/2}}.

Next, we have first that

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([0,\xi_{0}],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\xi r)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([0,\xi_{0}],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}+O(\xi_{0}).

This follows from the bounds
|\beta(\xi)|\lesssim|\xi|
,
\|b_{n}\|_{L^{2}}=1
and the
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)\to L^{2}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
boundedness of the maps
f(r)\mapsto\int f(r)J_{0}(r\xi)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
,
f(r)\mapsto\int f(r)J_{1}(r\xi)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
(two-dimensional Plancherel theorem). By the decomposition above,

\psi^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi^{2}(\xi,r)=(2+O(|\xi|))J_{0}(r\xi)+O(|\xi|)\frac{\cos(\xi r)}{\langle\xi r\rangle}+O(|\xi|)\frac{\sin(\xi r)}{\langle\xi r\rangle}+\psi_{\diamondsuit}^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi_{\diamondsuit}^{2}(\xi,r).

This implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\xi r)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([0,\xi_{0}],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\underbrace{\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}(\psi^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi^{2}(\xi,r))b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([0,\xi_{0}]\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}}_{\displaystyle=0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+|\xi_{0}|\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\cos(\xi r)}{\langle\xi_{0}r\rangle^{1/2}}b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([0,\xi_{0}],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}+|\xi_{0}|\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(\xi r)}{\langle\xi_{0}r\rangle^{1/2}}b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([0,\xi_{0}],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}(\psi_{\diamondsuit}^{1}(\xi,r)-\psi_{\diamondsuit}^{2}(\xi,r))b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([0,\xi_{0}],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}

We now claim that all the terms on the right-hand side are
O(\xi_{0})
. For the last term, this simply follows from
\|\psi(\xi,r)\|_{L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)}\lesssim 1
. For the second line in the above equation, this follows from the boundedness from
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
L^{2}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
of the map
f(r)\mapsto\int f(r)\frac{\cos(\xi r)}{|\xi r|^{1/2}}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
(one-dimensional Plancherel theorem) as well as the boundedness of the map
f(r)\mapsto\int f(r)\chi(\xi r)\cos(\xi r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
(which follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality).

Overall, we proved that

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([0,\xi_{0}],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\xi_{0}.

Step 4: High frequencies.
Since
|\beta(\xi)-1|\lesssim\frac{1}{\xi}
, we find by the same arguments as were used for low frequencies that

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([\xi_{1},\infty],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([\xi_{1},\infty],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}+O\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\right)

By Theorem
1.4
, we can decompose

\psi(\xi,r)=c_{0}(\xi)J_{1}(\xi,r)e(\xi)+c_{1}(\xi)(1-\chi(r))\frac{\sin(\xi r)}{\sqrt{\xi r}}e(\xi)+c_{2}(\xi)(1-\chi(r))\frac{\sin(\xi r)}{\sqrt{\xi r}}e(\xi)+\psi_{\spadesuit}(\xi,r)

with
|1-c_{0}(\xi)|+|c_{1}(\xi)|+|c_{2}(\xi)|\lesssim\frac{1}{\xi}
and

|\psi_{\spadesuit}(\xi,r)|\lesssim\frac{1}{\xi(1+r^{2})}.

We can now proceed in a similar way to what was done for low frequencies: using in particular the boundedness of
f\mapsto\int J_{0}(r\xi)f(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
and
f\mapsto\int f(r)\frac{\cos(r\xi)}{\sqrt{r\xi}}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r
from
L^{2}(r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
to
L^{2}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)
, we find that

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{1}(\xi r)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([\xi_{1},\infty],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}=\underbrace{\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}(\psi^{2}(\xi,r)-\psi^{1}(\xi,r))b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([\xi_{1},\infty],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}}_{\displaystyle 0}+O\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\right).

Overall, this means that

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}([\xi_{1},\infty],\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\lesssim\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}.

Conclusion of the argument.
Combining the contributions of small, medium and large frequencies gives a proof of the following limit

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)b_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Following the same argument and using in addition that

\left|\frac{\langle\xi\rangle}{|\xi|}(e^{1}(\xi)-e^{2}(\xi))\right|\sim 1\qquad\mbox{for any $\xi$}

gives the assertion on
(a_{n})
:

\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{0}(\xi r)+\frac{\beta(\xi)}{\langle\beta(\xi)\rangle}J_{1}(\xi r)\right)a_{n}(r)r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\xi\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi)}\overset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Writing
u_{n}
as the sum of its even and odd parts gives the desired statement.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

We define the kernel for
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
:

K_{0,1}(\xi,\eta)=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{0}(\xi r)J_{1}(\eta r)e^{-\varepsilon r}r\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r.

(1)

If a function
f\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathbb{R})
satisfies

|f^{\prime}(x)|+|f(x)-1|\lesssim\frac{1}{\langle x\rangle}

then the operator

T_{1}(g)(\eta)=\int_{0}^{\infty}(f(\xi)-f(\eta))K_{0,1}(\xi,\eta)g(\eta)\eta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta
